SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday up $1,760 on four trades across four different stocks as market conditions remained choppy and slow into the week's close. Starting with BATL, an energy stock benefiting from rising oil prices, Ross attempted a break through $2.75 seeking room to the all-time high of $3.40, but the breakout failed and he took a loss. PRSO appeared with news about a military drone identification system and though Ross caught the move from $1.48 to $1.64, he managed the position poorly, exiting for only $47 in profit. INDO provided the day's most substantial win, a $3,100 profit on a $6.20 entry that squeezed to $6.75 as oil momentum continued. The day peaked with SYNX (military/tactical systems), where Ross was up nearly $4,800 midday after entries at $1.50-$1.80, but a sharp reversal and his indecision about re-entering a position that had failed cost him the majority of those gains. By mid-afternoon, Ross recognized the market's lack of followthrough and the risk of further deterioration, choosing to step aside after the SYNX loss rather than chase additional setups in deteriorating conditions. The session illustrated the challenge of trading in a grinding market where even quality catalysts (military theme, oil strength) failed to produce consistent momentum.

SUMMARY:
Ross prepared his Monday morning watchlist following an unprecedented surge in oil prices—up 17 percent since Friday and approaching historically high levels not seen since 2022. The geopolitical conflict driving the energy rally created a thematic focus where defense and military-related stocks emerged as the week's primary catalyst, even more compelling than typical energy plays. BATL remained Ross's top watch despite Friday's choppy action, with elevated short interest (447 percent borrow cost, 73 percent utilization) suggesting squeeze potential, though price divergence versus USO and Friday's poor trade execution gave him pause. Secondary energy names like TMDE and ATET, despite benefiting from oil catalysts, had failed to sustain new highs, creating uncertainty about follow-through. In the biotech space, ATRA had gapped up 58 percent after-hours with reasonable float (4.8M), but Ross noted his historical difficulty trading moves that begin outside regular hours, preferring to wait for confirmation at the open. Tesla and other mega-cap gappers were rejected due to wide spreads and execution challenges unsuited to his trading approach. The overall market was pulling back 2 percent on the S&P despite oil strength, setting a cautious backdrop. Ross emphasized his focus on quality setups between $5-$10, anticipating that if the right combination of low float, catalyst, and chart pattern aligned, big moves could materialize, but he remained content to be selective rather than chase every opportunity.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday up $6,929 in a frustrating session that perfectly illustrated the danger of revenge trading and FOMO when a day begins well. Opening with NASN, the leading gapper at 7 a.m., he made a quick $1,145 scalp but missed the larger move that followed. The real opportunity came with OSRH, a recent IPO that had run hard on Wednesday and was repeating intraday around 9:26 a.m. Ross entered a starter position at the break of previous high ($4.30), added into the move to $5.30, and captured approximately $16,493 across two separate trade windows, bringing him to nearly $18,000 on the day by mid-morning. However, MLGO then emerged as a chart-perfect flat-top breakout setup just as Ross was approaching his $20,000 hot-market daily goal. He entered the first breakout attempt at $3.50, only to see it fail; stopped out, he re-entered the second breakout which also failed for a $3,000 loss. Most importantly, after being up $18,000 total, he gave into FOMO and capitulation emotions, taking a third trade on MLGO at $5.00 with 16,000-24,000 shares seeking a halt squeeze. The stock flushed to $4.60 then recovered, but he lost approximately $6,000-$7,000 on the add-back, turning a nearly 90 percent drawdown from his day's high into a $6,929 final profit. Ross emphasized this as a critical lesson: after a significant giveback, the urge to chase or revenge trade for bigger gains to "make up" the loss is one of the most dangerous psychological traps, and walking away while still green is a major discipline win.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday with $640 in profit, a small green day that he was grateful to secure given the market's volatility and the difficult close he navigated. The morning started with TISK, a five-cent tick stock that limited his ability to use automated hotkeys and forced manual order entry, which he found frustrating and limiting. He entered on a micro pullback near $5.00, saw it pop to $5.20, and managed a $450 profit before exiting, though the stock's price action felt constrained by its five-cent increment structure. The real lesson came from FFHL, a high-day momentum scanner stock he caught after it surged from $2.76 to $3.40 in early trading. Ross jumped in aggressively at $3.11-$3.20, adding heavily into the move as it squeezed to $3.40. However, what appeared to be simple breakout momentum suddenly reversed sharply, dropping all the way to $2.65—a flush of nearly 25 percent in seconds. Rather than panic-sell at the low, Ross waited for a bounce, exited near breakeven as FFHL recovered to $3.16, avoiding a panic loss. The stock continued higher after his exit, but he chose not to chase. The day's highlight was his recognition of the unrealized danger: at one point he received an alert that he was down more than $2,500 on a position, which would have forced him to reduce trading activity under his risk management rules. That near-call, combined with finally exiting in the green, left him grateful and disciplined enough to resist overtrading for larger gains on a day where momentum was fragile. His year-to-date profit now exceeded $364,000, and despite August being historically slow, he focused on grinding out small consistent wins rather than forcing bigger trades.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Wednesday down $5,000 (and back to red on the month) in an emotionally charged session that exemplified the danger of forcing trades in a cold market and giving into FOMO. Starting well with APOP, a recent reverse split gapping up 37 percent, he captured a solid $970 scalp on a 1-minute pullback entry near the $3.71 level, scaling at the VWAP test near $4.00. On OPGN (which had worked the prior day), a secondary offering wiped out overnight gains, and he exited a small dip-buy attempt for a $100 loss. The day's unraveling began with PTSDB, where after his initial $1,300 day profit, Ross saw a seemingly clean five-minute bull flag breakout around $4.00 and became too aggressive. He added three times ($3,000 → $6,000 → $9,000 shares) expecting continuation, only to watch the stock fizzle, drop to $3.75, recover briefly, and then fade, costing him $2,000. Frustrated by the $1,000 swing and now breakeven, Ross then chased SLGG on a false impulse to "make back" the loss by entering another breakout setup at $3.87, adding aggressively at higher levels, expecting a halt squeeze. When the stock rejected at $3.90 and dropped, Ross panic-exited for a $2,200 loss, losing $2,500+ per trade on his last two setups. By day's end, after starting with a $1,300 cushion, he was down nearly $4,200. Ross noted the core issue: he had traded a hot-market strategy (larger sizes, chasing breakouts for 50-75 cent moves, holding for bigger wins) in a cold market where these setups failed repeatedly. His inverted profit-loss ratio for the month reflected this mismatch, and he recognized the emotional trigger of revenge trading—trying to compensate for losses rather than accepting small baseline days. The session sent him back to red on August (his historically weakest month) and highlighted the importance of position sizing discipline.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished the Friday after Christmas up just over $5,000 on a single trade in a light-volume session where he had very low expectations coming in. Looking at his phone that morning at 6:30 a.m., he saw only a 30 percent leading gapper, which signaled a likely slow holiday-week market. Rather than skip the session, he got up and monitored the scanners. AMCI had been popping on the scanners intermittently throughout the prior week, and this morning it gapped up and began squeezing higher around $8.75. Ross analyzed the daily chart, identifying resistance at $10.40 and $12.47, with potential room back to $15.75. When AMCI started consolidating around $10.28 in the pre-open period, he began adding: entries at $10.28, $10.40, $10.50, and $10.75, building to an average of approximately $10.57. The stock squeezed to $11.00, where Ross took half his position off the table. It dipped, came back up, broke through $11.00 again, and he sold more, though he held through the final push expecting a test of the $12 high. The stock pulled back, tested an ascending support line near $11.40, and Ross watched it with buy orders queued above $11.40-$11.50 but never got in on the second move. Though he had wanted a bigger move and profit on that continuation stock, he recognized that in a holiday-week, low-volume environment, a base hit that achieved his cold-market daily goal of $5,000 was exactly what the situation called for. He closed satisfied with the discipline of taking his target and not overextending in a market lacking momentum, noting that similar slow conditions were likely through the remainder of the holiday period.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Monday up $55,000 on four trades, with the bulk—$43,000—coming from one high-volatility opportunity. GCTK had surged 200 percent after-hours on Friday without explanation and continued squeezing premarket. Ross took a small breakeven entry at the 6:55 a.m. break of $12, but the real trade came during the 10:35 a.m. pullback when he recognized that attention was shifting between GCTK and HSDT (the day's leading catalyst). As traders rotated from HSDT back into GCTK, Ross sized up expecting a squeeze back through the prior high, capturing about $6,353. HSDT was the day's primary mover—a biotech/medical device company that gapped from $9 to $19 on FDA/approval-related news at 7 a.m. Ross entered on an initial micro pullback at $12.80-$13.11 and scaled out as it ripped to $15, netting about $10,000. However, he made a critical error: on a second dip around $13, believing the stock would break higher, he added 25,000 shares aggressively expecting a continuation. Instead, the stock flushed hard, eventually testing lower levels, and his late add-back cost him approximately $15,000 of his earlier profit. The resulting late-exit loss on an otherwise quality trade demonstrated the risk of over-sizing on second entries when the first tranche was already profitable. SABS and MEIP provided smaller base hits ($5,000 and $500) on opening momentum and micro pullback entries. By late morning, after the HSDT setback, Ross had recovered to $50,000+ and eventually finished $55,000 higher. The session highlighted the importance of respecting profit targets and recognizing when early winners should not be re-risked on weaker follow-up setups, especially when the overall market shows signs of fading (HSDT's inability to hold above VWAP late-morning being a key warning).

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday with $568 in profit, a modest but meaningful win in a choppy market where he made a conscious decision to adapt his trading intensity to match conditions rather than force size and aggression. Starting with SLS from the gap scanner, he attempted a clean whole-dollar breakout at $2.00 only to see it fail and fizzle, costing him $700. Rather than chase additional momentum plays on a day when nothing was generating followthrough, he demonstrated discipline by reducing his share count targets to lighter sizes. MTSL, a continuation stock that had run the prior day, came into play when it tested the daily high of $3.70 on the charts. Ross accumulated 12,500 shares across five separate 2,500-share orders at $3.80, $3.85, $3.90, $3.94, and $4.00, buying the dips as it moved toward the breakout. When it reached $4.10, he scaled out half at $4.10-$4.11 and then continued selling the remaining position at progressively lower prices as the stock stalled, ultimately capturing $1,000 in profit despite the lack of clean breakout followthrough. Most notably, on CSLT—a rare short trade for Ross—he recognized a micro pullback false breakout pattern on the $5 level and shorted 16,000 shares at $5.50. As the stock dropped to $5.35-$5.40, he covered for a $240 gain, illustrating his flexibility in recognizing setups outside his primary long-bias approach. By 11:17 a.m., with only $568 in total profit and the market clearly not delivering momentum for larger trades, Ross withdrew from trading rather than push for additional entries. Later that afternoon, DHCP did gap and squeeze as a halted stock, moving from $5.50 to $7.60, but Ross correctly recognized that by the time he was reviewing it (at 11:17), his edge for the day had passed and the best move was disciplined patience. His lesson: on choppy, slow days, reducing share size, taking quick scalps, and cutting the session short when conditions don't support big runs is a sign of maturity, not weakness.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Wednesday down $5,859—a devastating session that saw him turn a $2,000 midday cushion into a $5,800+ loss, exemplifying one of his emotional trading triggers. The morning started with AWXT gapping up on a halt resumption. Ross entered the break of $5.10, captured a brief squeeze to $5.23, and took a quick $186 scalp as the stock fizzled, demonstrating the low-volatility, choppy nature of the day. ADMA had earnings news and came off the gap scanner, moving from $6.00 to $6.30+. Ross entered the break-through, added moderately, and took half off at $6.17-$6.20 before holding through a pullback and exiting higher, netting $296 on a small position. BPI showed strong technical strength: it had earned that morning and squeezed from $10 to $12. Recognizing strength, Ross took an unusual short entry at $11.20-$11.21 on the spike, stopped out with a small $250 profit, then pivoted to the long side at $10.77 on a pullback, scaled into the push toward $12.00, and exited for another $360 gain. By midday, Ross was up approximately $2,000 total. However, BSPM then emerged as a stock that had popped up and pulled back, consolidating around $2.50. When it broke $2.50, Ross initially waited for the break above $2.50 and added at $2.69. The stock continued squeezing to $3.00, and Ross took half off at $2.99. Rather than take his $2,000 profit for the day and walk, Ross spotted what appeared to be a setup building for another breakout through the prior daily high around $3.50. He aggressively added 7,500 shares at $3.30-$3.42, expecting a halt squeeze to $3.50+. Instead, in a single minute candle, BSPM collapsed from $3.43 to $2.63—a violent 80-cent washout. Caught badly, Ross initially averaged down trying to hold through, added at further lows, but ultimately stopped out for approximately $7,000 in losses. This green-to-red flip (losing 70 percent of his day's profit) triggered significant emotional activation. Ross described this as "the worst feeling" in trading—watching profit evaporate and feeling helpless. He admitted to being on the verge of revenge trading again, but ultimately recognized the danger and walked away while still slightly positive on the week. The session taught him that on choppy, lower-volume market days (typical for late July), reducing size, taking base hits, and walking away after losses are paramount.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Wednesday (with Thursday and Friday off for the holiday) up just over $800 on a single trade, well below his daily goals but reflecting his disciplined decision to avoid forcing setups in a thin market lacking momentum. The morning began with MWG, a penny stock gapping 120 percent, which Ross immediately dismissed as too cheap for his trading style. INDP had spiked after-hours but pulled back premarket, and Ross saw no compelling entry. By 7 a.m., his scanners showed nothing exciting, consistent with the post-Christmas week doldrums. Around 9 a.m., TNMG hit the scanners and began squeezing higher. Ross identified a micro pullback opportunity, entered the dip around $3.40 (as the stock was consolidating below the $3.50 resistance level), and as it bounced back up, took his profit into the move toward $3.50-$3.55, exiting for approximately $800. Although the stock continued higher after his exit, reaching $3.55+, he made no attempt to re-enter or chase the move, content to have captured a base hit in tough conditions. Later that morning, DRMA emerged with private placement news but Ross, based on prior experience losing money on this stock, immediately rejected the setup. OBR also appeared but didn't generate conviction on the premarket chart. Ross correctly recognized that the holiday-week environment—combined with a colder overall market lacking FOMO and exuberance—meant opportunities would be limited and small. Rather than oversize or chase lower-quality setups, he accepted the modest $800 win and refocused on waiting for January, when stronger technical themes and broader market momentum typically return. His overall message: discipline includes knowing when the market is not conducive to aggressive trading, and sitting tight with a small win rather than forcing size to chase bigger targets is the sign of a mature trader.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Monday down $3,000 on a single trade, marking a setback that he recognized stemmed from poor discipline and FOMO during a volatile opening session. PRPO first appeared on his scanner around 10:55 a.m. at $2.50 with modest momentum signals, but Ross initially underestimated it, hesitant about trading at that time of day. As the stock continued climbing to $3.45, he watched it move higher without him and fell victim to classic FOMO, jumping in at $3.40—far above the initial scanner alert. Rather than cut the loss when the stock pulled back, he added multiple times as it bounced, pushing his position from 3,000 to 6,000 to 9,000 to eventually 15,000 shares, averaging up as the price climbed through $3.90. The stock briefly reached a high of $3.93 before encountering a wall of hidden sell orders at that level and reversing sharply. Unable to get filled at his target exits, he stopped out of a massive 15,000-share position with a $3,000 loss. PRPO would go on to finish the day up 54 percent, a painful miss that highlighted Ross's struggle with lower-priced stocks and his tendency to chase moves past ideal entry windows. He emphasized being down $3,000 equated to losing one and a half days of his typical $2,000 daily profit target, leaving him frustrated but determined to execute better discipline tomorrow.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Thursday red by $1,910 on four trades, marking his first losing day in several days and a session he attributed to both market conditions and his own trading discipline slipping. He opened with AIM, anticipating a red-to-green move from the opening sell-off, making a quick $470 scalp as the stock bounced. NSP followed with a high-day momentum scanner hit where he caught the breakout near $4.20 with significant sell-wall rejection, capturing $540 on a clean breakout scalp. The day's potential turned sour with WWR (formerly a leading gapper that he had on watch). After entering at $4.75-$4.90 with roughly 3,000 shares expecting a move toward $4.50-$5.00, the stock inexplicably halted mid-session going down. When it resumed, it opened significantly lower, trapping him with losses of approximately $1,700 on what should have been a profitable gap-and-go trade. CLWT was a final small loss after jumping in on high-day momentum that failed to continue. Most significantly, Ross received news mid-session that his primary broker, Sure Trader (an offshore Bahamas-registered firm), had been suspended by the Securities Commission for five days—a reminder about counterparty risk with small offshore brokers. Rather than panic, he noted this reinforced his practice of regularly withdrawing profits and never leaving large balances with smaller brokers. His daily loss of $1,910 pushed him slightly red on the week despite strong prior days, but he emphasized that volatility and broker issues were secondary to his own trading errors—particularly the oversized position that couldn't exit quickly on WWR. He committed to better execution and tighter risk management going into Friday.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished day seven of his trading month up $1,136 at a California office location, continuing to build his daily average and demonstrating the importance of discipline even after disappointing prior days. He flew from Connecticut to California overnight following yesterday's losses, arriving tired and frustrated after a turbulent flight where technical issues with his traveling trading station delayed the open until 9:25 a.m. Despite suboptimal conditions—feeling tired, missing the YouTube watch list stream, frustrated setup—he executed two profitable trades. AMRH was the gap scanner leading gapper that gapped to the open and dropped sharply (down $4 per share from $12.90 to $10.36), triggering his typical pattern of waiting for the first candle to make a new high before exiting. He waited for confirmation, then sold the move up to $13.29-$13.49, capturing $453 in profit and turning a $4,000 midtrade loss into a winner. TNDM, a high-day momentum scanner hit, tested $4.00 support near $3.80 and popped back to $3.98, netting a $682 scalp. Rather than push for more trades in choppy, slower market conditions—the market was described as slightly choppy with limited continuation—he decided to exit early with $1,136 green, recognizing that this satisfied his new daily goal and that market conditions didn't warrant aggressive additional risk. His disciplined decision to step aside rather than chase setups mid-morning during chop demonstrated emotional maturity, and he noted his year-to-date average had dropped from $4,900 to $4,300 daily, still well-positioned for his half-million-dollar annual profit target. This trade up to $365,000 on the month, over one-third of the way to his million-dollar small account challenge.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Thursday (his sixth trading day and 11th consecutive green day) up $6,535, his best day of the year, though tempered by significant aggression on the third trade that gave back nearly half his morning profits. He started with CLPS, a previous runner he'd watched closely, entering as it broke the descending resistance line at $6.38 with first-minute conviction, scaling out at $6.99-$7.04 as it squeezed toward resistance for a $2,600 profit. The major winner came with SAEX, a gap-scanner stock that opened high but remained restricted due to volatility (100 percent margin restriction), limiting him to cash-only buying power. Despite starting his account at only $46,000 (intentionally reduced to simulate typical trader accounts), he managed the constraint well, buying into micro-pullbacks at $2.88-$2.94 and selling strength at $7.06-$7.18, capturing approximately $6,228 on roughly 3,000-4,000 shares. By 10 a.m., he was up nearly $9,000, prompting him to set higher daily goals. OPGN, a high-day momentum scanner hit, appeared at $1.95 with obvious momentum toward the $2.50 resistance level (the 200-day moving average). Ross aggressively sized up to 15,000 shares entering at $1.95, expecting a squeeze to $2.50 and beyond, adding 3,000-6,000 shares each as it moved. However, after briefly hitting $2.03, it reversed sharply and dropped to $1.83, forcing him to stop out at $1.80-$1.77 for a $2,300 loss. He noted this loss, while significant, was partially offset by the quality of his morning execution and was disciplined enough not to revenge trade afterward, ending the day up $6,535 and maintaining his "green day" streak, though he acknowledged the temptation to chase back losses as a behavioral warning sign.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Thursday up $4,000, a profitable day driven primarily by one penny stock trade (MMMD) that demonstrated both opportunity and challenge with ultra-cheap stocks. MMMD, a medical device stock, popped up at 7:00 a.m. gapping from $0.50 to $0.70 then squeezing to $0.80 in pre-market. Ross entered at $1.25 as it broke through $1.20-$1.30 resistance, scaled back at $1.30-$1.35 (first exit), and re-entered as it dipped to $1.48, ultimately riding the parabolic squeeze from $1.25 all the way to a high of $1.65. The stock went up over 225 percent in approximately 2 hours, and Ross captured approximately $4,000 on the move despite acknowledging his caution about position sizing on penny stocks due to commissions and ECN fees eating 10+ percent of small profits. He noted that trading 150,000 total shares with roughly 40 tickets and $450 in fees meant his net profit was actually closer to $3,800—a reminder that ultra-cheap stocks require either larger moves or significantly larger position sizes to justify trading costs. CTO and ASNS were secondary trades that provided minimal additional profit/loss. Most significantly, Ross observed that penny stock volume and volatility were clearly in focus with multiple 100+ million-share volume days, and while these stocks could move 200-300 percent, the execution and fee burden meant they were better for "set and forget" swing traders than his typical active scalping style. He noted the broader market was choppy with the leading 10-16 percent gap scanner being historically weak, suggesting he would likely see more penny stock-driven opportunities and fewer traditional technical setups through this volatile penny stock phase.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday up $5,659, his slowest day of the week but still comfortably above his $2,000 daily goal, as market conditions reflected broader political and macroeconomic shifts following the Trump election victory. The stock market rallied hard post-election with the S&P 500 making new all-time highs (reaching 597), and Tesla surging 27 percent in three trading sessions on Trump-administration tailwind sentiments. Ross took fewer trades than usual (only one for significant profit) as he observed that news catalysts were weak and many traditional momentum setups were "not obvious enough." His sole trade was VMAR, a continuation play that had gapped significantly after-hours on Friday and was repeating momentum into Monday morning. He entered the break of VWAP (volume weight average price) at $3.75-$3.90, added as the stock pushed higher through $4.10, and scaled out across multiple resistance levels, capturing approximately $5,659 in total profit as it squeezed toward $4.50 resistance. Ross noted that despite strong technical setup execution, the day felt methodical and slow compared to previous high-volume, multi-catalyst days, and he made a conscious decision not to chase secondary stocks when the primary momentum was clearly fading. He emphasized that while his one-trade profit was solid, this represented underperformance versus his better weeks and demonstrated the importance of quality catalyst news driving his best days. His broader reflection centered on the Trump administration and policy implications (higher tariffs affecting Chinese stocks negatively, renewable energy companies struggling due to Trump's pro-oil stance, small-cap Russell 2000 strength on domestic-first policies), and he acknowledged this macro backdrop would likely shift which sectors and stocks produced the best opportunities going forward.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Wednesday up approximately $13,400, part of a $103,000+ week, on an extremely volatile penny stock session where attention was sharply dispersed across multiple 100+ percent gainers competing for trader focus. CYN was his primary stock, hitting the high-day momentum scanner after gapping on NVIDIA-related news. He took three separate trades on CYN: a first at $5.25 scalping a quick $3,500 into the $6.40 spike; a second entry at $6.00 for a break back over VWAP that flushed back to $3.50, costing $3,000; and a recovery entry at $5.60 that rebounded to $6.00 for $3,300. BZI was his largest winner of the session, a curl-pattern trade where he caught the retest of the morning spike high, rode BZI from $2.40 through $3.40-$3.80, locking up $10,000 gross before giving back $3,000 near the top on a failed VWAP trade, netting approximately $7,000. GNLN followed a similar curl/dip-recovery pattern for $2,600. The session's defining challenge was WBUI—a penny stock with 500+ million shares of volume—dispersing attention away from quality setups like CYN and BZI, preventing them from achieving larger percentage moves they might have seen in isolation. Ross explicitly chose not to trade WBUI as he dislikes ultra-low-priced stocks requiring massive share counts. The week's running total reached $113,366 by session close.

Ross finished Tuesday up $8,390 (falling short of his double-goal target due to overtrading and emotional challenges mid-session) on four trades from the pre-market continuation action that was proving much slower than the explosive penny stock days of prior weeks. IMNN was his biggest winner, a stock that was up 18 percent pre-market as a continuation play from a massive previous day where it ran from $2.00 to $3.50+. Ross caught the 7:00 a.m. volume spike when brokers came online, jumping in at $3.11 after an initial failed micro pullback. He then actively traded micro pullbacks, buying dips and scaling partial exits as the stock moved through $3.20, $3.29, $3.40, and tested toward $3.50, ultimately capturing $3,917 despite the stock feeling "thickly traded" and choppy. CNSP was a quick $297 scalp on a continuation move that failed spectacularly later (dropping 50+ percent after his exit), highlighting how continued volatility made risk management crucial. TWOU was a gap-fill trade following the previous day's halt and release, capturing a small $206 scalp as support held. Most significantly, PCSA emerged with news at 8:00 a.m. and initially moved higher before stalling at $2.90 resistance. Ross was up approximately $4,200 on this trade at its peak (micro pullback entries at $2.20 and subsequent entries at $2.55, $2.70, $2.85), but he committed a classic overtrading error: after the first successful micro pullback exit netted profits, he continued re-entering the same stock repeatedly for the breakout of $3.10, taking losses on each failed attempt ($600, another $600, another $600), ultimately giving back approximately $1,700 of his $4,200 gain. By 11:00 a.m. after more unsuccessful setups on smaller stocks (SCV, LN), he recognized he was "trading his P&L" emotionally and walked away at $8,390. The session exemplified Tuesday's slower conditions compared to weekend/Monday penny stock fireworks, and he acknowledged his frustration at being up nearly $10,000 then giving it back through poor discipline and emotional trading decisions.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Wednesday up $36,000, his largest winner coming from MEGL (a stock up over 100 percent by his entry), but the day highlighted the importance of recognizing when attention becomes dispersed across too many competing movers. MEGL was a curl-pattern revival entry where the stock initially popped to $1.50 pre-market, pulled back significantly, then curled back up as he watched the pattern repeat. He jumped in at $3.00 (the break of the previous spike high) and added as momentum built, riding the stock from $3.00 all the way to nearly $4.00, scaling out progressively as each micro pullback was bought. He captured approximately $30,000 on this single trade through disciplined size and multiple exit points. EVGN provided the second quality setup: a news-driven pop where the stock broke $2.00 resistance early, and Ross entered at $1.88 anticipating the breakout. As the stock moved to $2.30-$2.40, he scaled out partial profits, though he noted giving back $2,000 near the top when attempting to add into a failed break through a descending resistance line. The total for EVGN was approximately $7,900 after the giveback. GELS appeared briefly with news but failed to generate momentum, resulting in only a $112 loss on a small position. Most significantly, Ross noted that attention was being drawn across multiple penny stocks simultaneously: CARGO CARM (crypto-related, 300 million shares of volume), KLTO (yet another penny runner), and others meant that even strong stocks like MEGL didn't achieve the 300-400 percent moves seen in isolation. He explicitly stated that because dispersed attention was fragmenting the market across many movers, quality stocks underperformed relative to what they might achieve if they were the sole focus. His decision to exit after EVGN and sit on the sidelines reflected recognition that attempting to catch additional setups in a fragmented market was likely to generate more losses than wins, a mature disciplined approach to managing capital on complex market-structure days.

SUMMARY:
This educational episode teaching the five-step stock selection method focuses on identifying the strongest day-trading candidates from thousands of daily market options. Ross demonstrates how historical analysis of his 20+ million dollars in trading profits reveals consistent patterns in stocks that deliver 50+ percent daily gains, emphasizing that success comes from recognizing imbalances between supply (float) and demand (trading volume) driven by news catalysts. The five-step process consists of: (1) relative volume of 5x or higher above 50-day average (measured by tools like scanners that automatically filter); (2) potential for 25+ million shares of daily volume; (3) a gap-up of 2+ percent in pre-market versus prior close; (4) price range between $2-$20 per share where Ross demonstrates profitability concentrates; and (5) float under 10 million shares to enable explosive percentage moves. Ross uses NERVE stock (up 300+ percent on $200 million capital raise news for schizophrenia drug advancement) as his worked example, showing how the stock met all five criteria and enabled $11,000+ profit through multiple micro-pullback entries and exits using 10-second candlestick charts. He emphasizes that trading the strongest 100+ million-share-volume names (often biotech or penny stocks announcing catalysts) requires waiting for MACD confirmation on intraday charts and recognizing that most profit is captured in the first leg higher (typically before 11:00 a.m., with peak trading between 7:00-9:30 a.m. when news is released and brokers come online). The episode reinforces that successful small-account growth requires focus on volatility stocks with specific quantifiable criteria rather than trying to trade blue chips or larger-cap stocks that move only 2-4 percent daily, making percentage capture nearly impossible on small accounts.

He identified the stock's extremely low float (approximately 1 million shares) combined with massive volume (110 million shares) created a severe supply-demand imbalance driving a 350+ percent move. ZENA had completed an IPO earlier and the massive volume influx meant anyone underwater from earlier buyers could finally exit, yet the stock continued squeezing. He noted a negative MACD reading as the stock approached $8.00 in after-hours, signaling potential exhaustion, and wisely declined to chase further. While not a home-run trade, locking up a "little base hit" proved his philosophy that patience waiting for obvious setups combined with aggressive action when conditions align can turn an expected non-trading day into a profitable one. The session reinforced his focus on float rotation and relative volume as the primary indicators driving explosive moves rather than chart patterns alone.

SUMMARY:
This comprehensive educational episode explains Ross's five-pillar stock selection process that forms the foundation of his momentum day-trading strategy, designed to identify the strongest candidates from thousands of daily options. The key pillars are: (1) price range of $2-$20, ideally $5-$10 where smaller accounts can capture meaningful percentage moves; (2) already up 10+ percent filtering down from 8,000 potential stocks to 50; (3) high relative volume (minimum 5x) indicating exceptional activity versus the stock's average; (4) news catalyst providing the fundamental reason for the move; (5) float under 20 million shares creating explosive potential when supply-demand imbalances form. Ross contrasts small caps with large caps, noting that while large caps offer stability and leverage, they are dominated by high-frequency trading algorithms where retail traders struggle, whereas small caps between $2-$20 with low floats provide the volatility necessary for meaningful daily gains (10-50%+) that directly build small accounts. He uses CG (Cellectar Biosciences) as his worked case study: a recent IPO with 950K float doing a 12:1 reverse split, gapping up on a European patent for an ADHD treatment, eventually rising 161 percent from $1.80 premarket to nearly $3.00, netting Ross approximately $12,000 in profit within the first 30 minutes. His technical strategy emphasizes finding stocks early on his gap-scanner and high-day-momo scanner, then waiting for micro pullbacks on the one-minute chart to define entry points with tight stops, managing risk conservatively until building a daily cushion before sizing up. The episode stresses that consistent success requires discipline to follow the rules, practice in a simulator first, and accepting that 80+ percent of annual profits come from a small handful of hot days rather than grinding daily profits.

His overall thesis centered on waiting for 2-3 day strong moves before jumping in, rather than chasing day-one volatility, allowing chart patterns to set up more cleanly for second and third-day entry attempts. The week's success (four green days totaling approximately $11,000+) positioned him to potentially reach his reduced monthly goal of $40,000 if follow-through momentum continued.

NO-TRADE SUMMARY:
Ross took his second no-trade day of 2026, demonstrating his evolved approach to trading discipline where 40 percent fewer trades compared to 2024 resulted in three times the profit—a philosophy he actively implemented despite the psychological discomfort of inaction. Pre-market analysis revealed limited attractive candidates: MNTS had broken below its volume-weighted average price trend line before the 9:30 open; LUCY continued its frustrating pattern of popping and flushing repeatedly throughout previous sessions; GLUE offered easy-to-borrow short availability but insufficient volatility; AMOD priced too low for his trading style; and the leading gapper wasn't even up 50 percent before the market's 9:30 open. Rather than settling for marginal setups, Ross forced himself to stand aside, trusting his revised framework that acceptance of selective no-trade days during cold periods prevents the destructive pattern of revenge trading and low-quality entries during choppy action. He noted AZI and SUGP both gapped heavily but proved untradable on his primary broker (Schwab), reinforcing his frustration with broker restrictions that could block major moves and his recommendation that traders maintain backup accounts with brokers like Webull (offering recent IPO access and fewer restrictions). The session highlighted Ross's strategic evolution: recognizing that waiting through one or two cold days preserves capital for the inevitable hot days when multiple 10-50 percent moves appear simultaneously, creating the rare opportunities that likely drive 80+ percent of annual profits. His small account challenges on Webull and planned Robin Hood testing reflected his recognition that flexibility in broker choice directly impacts opportunity capture. For traders building small accounts, the lesson was clear: patience in cold markets, executed with confidence, beats forced action that transforms small losses into portfolio-damaging streaks.

SUMMARY:
This educational episode addresses the psychological foundation of consistent day trading profitability by contrasting profit-seeking mindset (which often leads to overtrading and revenge trading) with a process-focused mindset emphasizing discipline and risk management. Ross shares that his transformation from struggling early in his trading career came when he shifted focus from "how much can I make today" to "can I follow my rules and manage my risk correctly." He emphasizes that the goal isn't to hit a specific daily profit target but rather to rate each day's quality (1-10 scale) early in the session based on gap scanner strength, first few trades' outcomes, and overall market feel, then adjust strategy accordingly. Hot days (7-10 rating) warrant aggressive multi-trade positioning and hold-through-resistance approaches; neutral days (4-6) require selective single-trade focus; cold days (1-3) demand strict single-trade discipline or complete avoidance to avoid digging a hole through revenge trading. His personal metrics reveal that approximately 20 percent of trading days (roughly four per month in a 20-day month) generate 80 percent of annual profits—making it essential to avoid deteriorating during the 80 percent of days that are fundamentally slow or choppy. By eliminating the emotional attachment to daily goals and replacing it with process discipline, traders internalize the reality that missing some gains on slow days is preferable to the portfolio-killing losses that result from fighting choppy market conditions. Ross illustrates how his 90-day equity curve experiences drawdowns but recovers through patience, discipline, and consistent execution of quality-over-quantity principles, ultimately proving that the psychological work of accepting market limitations drives success more than technical skill alone.

SUMMARY:
Ross locked in $5,500+ profit in just 47 minutes on March 1st, March 2024's first trading day, demonstrating his belief that early-month aggression combined with strong pre-market momentum can offset his compressed morning due to school drop-off duties. MINM appeared as a pre-market gapper but with some uncertainty about its true catalyst, leading Ross to initially avoid it; however, as it demonstrated clean VWAP breaks and multiple halt sequences on the one-minute chart showing new highs, he engaged aggressively. His strategy employed systematic micro-pullback buying (at $6.42, $6.50, $7.11) with tight stops underneath whole-dollar levels, capturing approximately $1,300 across multiple small winners. ADIL emerged post-9:30 a.m. on his high-day-momo scanner, showing classic second-day follow-through after a shooting-star close the previous day. Despite his initial skepticism about the lack of fresh news catalyst, Ross observed the five-minute chart setting up beautifully with a clean pullback after the $2.30-$2.70 spike, and he capitalized aggressively by buying the ABCD consolidation pattern dip at $2.55, accumulating 4,000-6,000-share entries. The stock squeezed to $2.80-$2.90, netting him $4,200+ across multiple profit-taking exits as it approached the $2.95 200-moving-average resistance level. His rapid session completion by 7:47 a.m. (forced by family obligations) meant he avoided the market's typical late-morning chop, reinforcing his thesis that best execution occurs in the narrow 7:00-9:30 a.m. window where news and early scanner alerts create the cleanest price action for disciplined scalp traders.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished March 1st red $3,828 across five consecutive losing trades—a historic zero-percent accuracy day that epitomized the frustration of fighting a genuinely slow, choppy market despite being relatively early to his desk at 7:40 a.m. The session began poorly when he sat down to discover that JD Zeng had already completed its 50+ percent move premarket (from $5 to $11) on light volume, highlighting his frustration at missing early gaps. RAY, benefiting from recent IPO sympathy momentum alongside the big ZDG move, failed to materialize—Ross entered at $5.00 anticipating aggressive squeeze but stopped at $4.30 after the stock couldn't hold resistance and dropped to $4.50 baseline. BFRG, a previously traded beaten-down stock, triggered a false breakout where Ross got trapped above the level only to see the stock immediately sell off. LSDI, a genuine news catalyst with an ADHD treatment patent announcement at 7:50 a.m., provided what should have been an excellent setup: the stock gapped 37 cents, pulled back, then reversed sharply from $3.70 entry down to $3.15 and then to a low of $3.00. His largest loss came on ZENG (Chinese real estate company name similar to JD Zeng), where he employed his beloved curl-back-to-VWAP recovery pattern, entering near $5.30-$5.75 anticipating a $5.87+ pop—instead the stock hit a limit halt down, resuming lower at around $5.60, finally stopping him out for $1,100. By 9:15 a.m., down $3,828, Ross recognized the market's fundamental coldness (no clean leading gappers, high spreads on tight-float stocks, reversals on what appeared to be quality setups) and exercised the discipline to walk away rather than force trades. His historically perfect record broke on a day that perfectly illustrated why his year-to-date average remained below last year's—slow markets with elevated choppy price action simply do not reward the breakout momentum strategy; patience and selective avoidance prevents portfolio hemorrhaging.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday, the first trading day of May, with a modest $750 profit (after fees/commissions) despite having been up as much as $1,300 at high water mark and subsequently giving back half his gain. Market conditions remained persistently slow with limited quality gappers, forcing him to take what opportunities appeared rather than chase marginal setups. MNTS provided an early entry pre-market as it showed initial squeeze momentum but limited fuel, yielding only a quick $350 scalp. BZI, a grinder stock with 10M float and 63M shares of volume, presented multiple micro-pullback dip-buying opportunities in the tight $2.28-$2.32 range—he captured successive breakout attempts at the $2.30, $2.32, and $2.35 levels, accumulating an $1,100 profit despite the frustratingly thick order book that made the stock difficult to size-up trades (unable to scale beyond 10,000 shares due to market maker depth). His only loss came on NEXI (higher-priced at $7.34), a bounce-day continuation stock from yesterday's move that appeared clean but revealed multiple false breakouts and whipsaws at the $7.44 and $7.50 levels, stopping him out twice for a $700 combined loss. At approximately 12:30 p.m., having seen no further opportunities emerge from the gap scanner despite waiting through morning into early afternoon, Ross exercised discipline to walk away while still green rather than force additional choppy trades that historically lead to small-account death spirals. His 90-day equity curve showed recent recovery from a late-December drawdown, and his monthly goal of $40,000 remained achievable if market conditions warmed from this cold cold start to May. He noted that calendar-wise, a typical 20-trading-day May with just 10-15 decent opportunity days would likely produce 80+ percent of monthly profit on those few hot days, reinforcing the value of patient selectivity.

SUMMARY:
Ross endured his first red day in 22 consecutive trading days (ending an impressive July streak that began June 27th), finishing down $1,971.44 in summer conditions plagued by light volume and heavy spreads. The session's defining trade was BBLG, a stock that became his personal nemesis through three separate entry attempts, each revealing the peril of sizing up during slow markets and emotional frustration. His first BBLG trade entered at $3.20 for a flat-top breakout (expecting continuation), but the stock reversed hard; he held this oversized 20,000-share position into the open hoping for recovery, triggering a devastating $6,000 loss before finally exiting at $3.30. The 22-day green streak and new-month psychology likely influenced his willingness to take that much risk so early, compounded by missing the initial professional moves in premarket that set the tone. A brief middle-market scalp on BBLG clawed back $900, briefly returning him to break-even. His largest winner came on WINT (short interest 47%, cost-to-borrow 20 cents per share), which he monitored premarket as a potential short-squeeze candidate. When WINT opened at $11.25 and squeezed to $12+ on the opening halt, he participated in the secondary leg by scaling exits from $11.99, capturing $1,500—a rare bright spot in an otherwise frustrating day dominated by light volume, wide spreads, and failed setups on MDJH, IVP, NRSN, and BMEA. By 11:30 a.m. (hard stop for family obligations), Ross recognized the day's weakness and resisted the temptation to chase Tesla or Nvidia, acknowledging that desperation trading would only compound his loss. His max-loss system (set at $7,500 to protect recent hot-streak confidence) triggered near-maximum pain ($7,430 realized loss) mid-session before partial recovery to -$1,971, demonstrating how aggressive sizing during slowing markets—even with a hot streak mentality—can rapidly erode profits if discipline fails.

SUMMARY:
Ross recorded his first red day of November (third day of month) after a dominant 73-74 consecutive green-day streak through most of 2024, finishing down approximately $4,113 on three trades and experiencing both the frustration of a colder market and his own behavioral lapses in setup selectivity. LNAI, a biotech stock with 14M float and FDA news catalyst, appeared on scanners early but didn't perfectly fit his five-pillar stock-selection criteria (price slightly low, float slightly elevated). Despite these warning signs, he entered at $1.17 for a pullback-dip continuation and was stopped out for a $1,213 loss when the stock rejected below his entry. His emotional response to that initial loss directly led to his second mistake: re-entering LNAI at $1.44-$1.52 with two quick add-ons, seeking a squeeze through $1.55-$1.60, only to get stopped out a second time (down $2,900 combined across both LNAI trades). CCTG, a Hong Kong-listed Chinese company, was his opening trade: he caught the micro pullback at $1.65 after the stock squeezed from $1.00 to $1.70 in the opening minutes, but the stock reversed hard and dropped back down through his entry, trapping him for a $900 loss. By mid-morning, Ross recognized the pattern—three consecutive losses without any winners—and knew statistically the market was not in his favor. His market commentary revealed the psychological toll: he was frustrated, grinding against a colder-than-summer-2024 environment where crypto treasuries and biotech catalysts were not performing. Despite being up massively on the year (90-day equity curve solidly green, over $1M in gross profit for the year), the emotional difficulty of transitioning from hot to cold markets forced him to recognize that the best medicine was discipline (walking away) rather than revenge trading the remaining day.

SUMMARY:
Ross recorded his second consecutive Thursday red-day recap, finishing down $685.72 on seven different stocks, demonstrating that despite solid 75-80% trade accuracy (four winners out of seven traded stocks), poor profit-to-loss ratio and lack of large winners created net losses. His biggest loser was LGVN, which halted up at $3.00 and resumed with him long, only to reverse hard below his $2.85 support level, costing him $2,500 on a trade where he expected another halt or sustained squeeze given the short-interest dynamics. ZAP presented a similar halt-and-trap scenario where he got stopped out on a move back down before it bounced back—a pattern that plagued him throughout the morning. Gainers included small wins on DRCT (gapper), BRTX (thick buyer at $2.00 level supported a $948 scalp despite using oversized 15,000-share positions and incurring $200+ in commissions on just that trade), and three quick scalps on GME ($377 win on the shareholder-meeting hype). The session's defining challenge was GameStop: Ross spent the morning tracking WINT's short squeeze potential, noticed the open interest decline on June 21st calls suggesting a position change, and watched as the scheduled 10 a.m. shareholder meeting crashed due to unexpected traffic—delaying any catalyst catalyst spike that might have driven stronger follow-through on momentum names. BNED showed 40% gains by day's end, yet Ross captured nothing as it never completed a clean second-leg breakout. By 1:30 p.m., having cycled from green $1,300 at peak to red -$3,000 at trough before recovering to -$685, Ross exercised hard discipline by refusing to chase lower-quality setups, recognizing that small red days are successful days if they prevent cascading losses—a mindset adjustment that allowed him to call it while still managing risk effectively.

SUMMARY:
Ross started Monday, August 5th (second trading day of the month) with a significant challenge: he had just endured his first red day on Friday ($1,971 loss), and today he faced a market that remained sluggish and choppy. He remained disciplined by deliberately throttling back share size to quarter-capacity positions (holding at 5,000-share cap until profitable), which ultimately saved him from potentially devastating losses if he had traded full-size aggression. SNGX provided his best opportunity—news broke around 7:30 a.m., and the stock popped immediately. He entered at $4.00 for the $4 round-number break, observing clean VWAP reclaims and micro-pullback patterns that justified aggressive scaling on the way up, ultimately capturing $4,300 across multiple partial exits as the stock failed to sustain momentum above $4.30. VTA proved far more frustrating: the stock had news and initially pulled away from $7.55 before dropping sharply, then rebounding—multiple false breakouts at the $8.00 and higher levels trapped him three times (break-even entry, small win, then two consecutive losses for -$7,000 combined). By 8:30 a.m., having peaked at $4,300 profit, he faced the psychological test: continue trading hoping for mean-reversion or walk away while still profitable? Despite seeing SNGX curl back up around 10 a.m., he recognized that today's poor overall market quality (no hot gap scanner, no strong pre-market signals beyond SNGX) meant additional risk would likely exceed reward. His decision to log off while only down $2,869 (after throttled sizing) exemplified the maturity gained from Friday's $6,000 BBLG loss—he understood that defending small losses during slow markets beats chasing recovery trades during chop.

Monday, October 7th marked Ross's third red day of October (first Monday red day) and reflected the market's slow transition into autumn as volatility waned from summer highs. His opening trade on LC (gap-down reversal from $1.85 to $4.50) demonstrated his typical avoidance rule for such plays, yet desperation to break the ice after a slow pre-market (nothing good on scanners until 7:27 a.m.) led him to enter the recovery move anyway—capturing only +$21 on the scalp before the stock's subsequent halt-and-reversal trapped him for -$1,500 on the second entry. His max-loss discipline kicked in by 10:20 a.m. when cumulative losses reached -$2,500 (three losses: LC -$1,500, YXIA -$700, PEEV -$163, with only two break-even trades on GSI and the opening LC trade). Rather than risk doubling that loss mid-market, he recognized the broader pattern: weak gap scanner (only 52M-float 300% gainers, no clean small-cap opportunities), Chinese company traps (CHSN pump-and-dump from earlier in week still fresh on mind), and generally poor liquid setups. His internal debate—should I keep pushing to recover the loss?—was resolved by confronting statistics: he had 0% accuracy so far (three losses + two break-evens), and the probability of additional losses far outweighed recovery odds on a day with no strong sector momentum. This demonstrated his maturity in accepting "successful red days" when size is managed and losses are kept small despite multiple trades—far better than a day that spirals to -$5,000 or -$7,500 through revenge trading.

SUMMARY:
Ross's first red day of 2025 after a dominant 76-consecutive-day green streak (ending November 2024) delivered a visceral $17,372 loss—his largest single-day drawdown in over a year despite strong pre-market momentum with TN up 400% pre-market. The session exemplified how market extremes create both opportunity and peril: while overall sentiment remained hot (stocks up 300%+, daily moves substantial), execution difficulty proved treacherous because every big move attracted every trader, creating massive spreads and whipsaws. His opening trades on TN should have been cleanly profitable, but the stock's extreme thickness (175M shares of volume, difficult-to-read level-two due to sheer order flow) created confusion. After winning +$7,000 on a sequence of TN micro-pullback adds from $4.17 to $5.70, he made his catastrophic error: adding 15,000 shares at $5.70 (above his typical 5,000-share pre-$1K-cushion cap) anticipating a squeeze to $6.00+, only to watch the stock collapse within three minutes from $5.40 to $4.80, generating a one-trade loss of $18,000. His subsequent recovery trades (POI +$900, BLNE +$100, ALMS +$1,400 across two dip-buys) captured small base hits but revealed a critical insight: on extremely hot days, the market provides both mega-cap opportunities (TN, MLGO) and simultaneously penalizes aggressive sizing because volatility is unpredictable—support breaks violently, bounces overshoot. By 11:00 a.m., recognizing he had hit his revised +$20,000-maximum-risk line and maintained emotional composure despite the pain, he walked away. His post-session reflection acknowledged that hot-market aggression (which served him well in July-August) must be calibrated differently when markets show signs of turning: after six-figure green days, taking oversized risk on the next 300%+ move violates his own core risk-management principles.

SUMMARY:
Ross's second red day of 2025 (down $4,915.32 after fees) arrived just three trading days following his largest red day (-$17,372), reflecting psychological difficulty in recalibrating from recent over-losses and maintaining the required discipline after emotional trauma. LIMN, a recent IPO that had squeezed from $7 to $40 in after-hours, offered a blue-sky setup that looked textbook: pre-market retest at $26, pull back to $24, pop to $26, reverse below VWAP—Ross bought 4,500 shares anticipating a hold-and-squeeze to $37-$40, then added aggressively to 6,000 shares at $28 as it approached previous resistance. In seconds, it dropped to $26 and he lost $2 per share (-$15,786)—a reminder that even "perfect" technical setups fail when underlying momentum wanes. His immediate recovery came via AIHS (Chinese stock, lower-priced, highly volatile): he rode the squeeze from $1.00 to $1.73 capturing +$16,000 within minutes—statistically offsetting his LIMN loss and returning him to flat. His remaining trades revealed a pattern of overtrading post-large-loss: ZJK (+$5,000 peak → -$500 loss due to premature add-on), ZENA (+$5,000 peak → -$1,500 loss from oversized sizing), SCNI (+$5,000 peak → -$1,500 loss from continuation into pullback). Each of these began as winners yet became losses through his additions on extended moves rather than disciplined exit at target—a behavioral sign of frustration and desire to recoup the LIMN blow. His self-awareness recognized the shift: "higher expectations on what these would do versus what they actually did"—typical of a trader shell-shocked by big loss, seeking outsized reward. By early afternoon, sitting -$4,915 and recognizing the pattern, he deliberately walked away rather than chase further, embodying the discipline that small red days prevent catastrophic cascades.

SUMMARY:
Tuesday, May 27th delivered Ross a successful small red day of -$1,536.67, demonstrating that despite lacking large winners or impressive trade accuracy (1 winner, 1 break-even-ish, 1 major loser across three stocks), his discipline to keep losses manageable when market quality is poor represents mature risk management. EDBL kicked off the session with a +$190 scalp on an early pop that lacked follow-through—a sign of choppy market conditions ahead. SMFL proved to be the most frustrating trade: it had no clear news catalyst, squeezed from $6.50 to $7.50 in pre-market, and he entered on micro-pullbacks at the break of $7.00, capturing only +$29 total across multiple small entries and exits—thick order book, poor follow-through, and lack of buyer conviction meant even "clean" setups netted only pennies per share in profit. PIPE, the only stock with actual news (biotech headline), presented what looked like a solid dip-buy at $7.85 thinking it would break $8.00 and run toward $8.50+, but he added aggressively as the stock climbed to near $8.00, only to get whipsawed back down below his entries, triggering a -$1,800 loss. His peak equity on the day touched +$700-$800 after SMFL trades, yet he couldn't hold—by early afternoon, he sat -$1,200, -$1,500, finally settling at -$1,536 as remaining market was too choppy to offer quality setups (checked ATGL, SIFY, QSG—all light volume, wide spreads, no clean breakouts). His key insight: had he traded these same stocks at 10,000-15,000-share sizes (instead of his disciplined 5,000 cap), losses would have been 2-3x worse, and winners unchanged due to slippage—reinforcing that days without strong momentum require smaller size, not bigger size. By mid-afternoon, having exercised "medium well" discipline by walking away before reaching his -$1,000 max-loss floor, he recognized the trade-off: can't be green every day, but can stay green on the week and month by refusing to dig a hole on choppy days.

SUMMARY:
Ross's red day of -$387.44 on a choppy Thursday (described as "successful red day" due to managing risk on a morning that started down -$2,000 and peaked down -$3,000) demonstrated the psychological challenge of trading after a brutal three-trade opening and the discipline required to avoid catastrophic cascades. PHUN opened his day with a +$15 scalp on the morning gapper, establishing early confidence. However, KALA destroyed that confidence: he got in at $6.12 anticipating a micro pullback continuation (common pattern), but instead the stock flushed instantly to $5.60, triggering a -$1,700 loss on 5,000 shares and starting him down nearly -$2,000 immediately. GRII presented an offering-completion play at $1.96 that looked clean until big sellers arrived post-open, forcing a -$700 loss. By 8:30 a.m., he had endured three losses (-$1,700 KALA, -$700 GRII, plus the KALA dip recovery +$200) and sat down ~-$2,200 on a day with no winners yet. His internal debate—keep grinding for recovery or walk away?—was resolved when he caught small winners on BSFC (+$998) and POI (+$900) with micro-pullback scalps, climbing back toward break-even. GOVX nearly became a fourth loss: he got aggressive after KALA's initial stop, bought 2,000+ shares at $4.40 above the 200-moving-average anticipating breakout to $4.50+, then added at $4.03 (averaging down in panic), watched it bounce to $4.50, and exited break-even having experienced maximum emotional whip. MLGO, a continuation play that was halted down premarket, tempted him throughout the day (first pullback scalp -$500, second pullback scalp +$500, third pullback attempt -$387 final loss), representing the classic revenge-trade pattern where a trader's desire to "just hit flat" keeps them in the markets longer than prudent on low-conviction setups.

SUMMARY:
Friday of a short four-day holiday week (Memorial Day Monday closure) yielded Ross a small +$1,457.57 green day despite challenging conditions: typical Friday weakness, holiday-week liquidity shortage (institutional money on long weekends), and overall market pullback from recent highs. SMMT, a stock that squeezed from $8 to $10 overnight on 82M volume, looked strong but presented a critical challenge—his entry point was already extended ($9.50-$10.00), capturing only the top of the micro-pullback pattern; despite small winner (+$128), he missed the easy $2 move to begin with by chasing entry. NVOS, a break-above-$1.00 setup on what should have been a normal momentum gapper, flopped immediately with choppy whipsaws back below $1.00, stopping him out for -$227 on thin capital deployment. NN provided the day's highlight: a true blue-sky setup (pre-market high at $9.50 on pending news) that ripped at the open. He caught the scalp at $10.00, rode it to $10.50-$10.79, and executed a hot-key exit for +$800 before the stock's reversal—a textbook example of taking quick profits when entries are clean and immediate follow-through confirms conviction. By 10:30 a.m., having captured only +$1,457 across three names, recognizing the day's fundamental weakness (holiday week, no big catalysts, LPA halt with insane spreads indicating fragility, BNED up 40% but no participation available), he deliberately logged off rather than force setups. His end-of-week reflection acknowledged May's solid-but-unspectacular $40,000+ performance—not a home-run month, but consistent and green, embodying the philosophy that "80% of profit comes from 20% of trading days," making selective patience on slow weeks as valuable as aggression on hot days.

Opening with a subdued watchlist and dead gap scanner (MYOH only gapped 25%), Ross's calendar event—"stress-free trading"—reflected his psychological recovery goal rather than aggressive profit targets. Though he was frustrated by BOXL's lack of follow-through compared to IAMTE's continued squeeze to $10.10, his disciplined profit-taking on the sympathy play—avoiding revenge-trading pursuit of the original catalyst—earned him confidence and completed his week's recovery goal.

SUMMARY:
Ross's +$29,000 recovery day arrived after a devastating -$65K Friday, perfectly illustrating his "one-step-forward discipline" philosophy: only recover half the loss before sizing down, prevent stair-stepping cascades, and avoid emotional aggression. ACOR (second leading gapper) provided a small +$1K warm-up on three quick scalps ($0.75 break → $0.83 pop, exit; $0.65 break attempt → $0.74 pop; first candle new high → exits). LIVE (earnings catalyst: net income $6.40/share, $20M operating income increase) became his focus after observing the 4a.m gap-up to $10 upon news. Premarket chop ($21 entries at breakouts, $25 adds, $27-28 adds) gave way to open squeeze; his core 4,000-share position benefited from pops into halts, opening lower and popping again, capturing multiple +$1-2 per share scalps from $21 entry to $32-33 high, totaling +$32K. QLI initially worked (breakout scalp at $5.78 → $9.00 pop, +$500 quick win) but reversed as he went for the "perfect" dip at $5.80 (re-entry add), expecting continuation; instead, it whipsawed into -$2K loss—the classic "already made money, let's get perfect" revenge pattern. His peak was +$60K midday, yet he gave back $30K across QLI whipsaw and VUZI small loss. Sitting -$29K positive (versus -$65K Friday), he consciously logged off, closed everything, recognizing the discipline: "I don't want to be tempted. Out of sight, out of mind." His note: trading with smaller size (6K-share caps that day) meant losses stayed 30K giveback instead of potentially 60K+ on bigger position catastrophes.

SUMMARY:
Tuesday morning delivered Ross a +$6,000 green day in a "coldest market" environment, with crypto-correlated drag (Bitcoin heading toward 20K support, major volatility collapse) forcing extreme selectivity and near-rejection of otherwise textbook setups due to market participant behavior including order-spoofing algorithms. BNRG (Israeli energy stock, sub-1M float, pre-market reverse-split candidate) opened at $3.80 below VWAP after 4a.m pop; he targeted VWAP reclaim for a bounce, entered $4.15, added $4.25, $4.50, and achieved a quick +$272 scalp on VWAP pop without follow-through. RVSN (Israeli biotech, repeat scanner popper) emerged at $8.03 with gap up on news, but his level-2 showed 50-cent to $1+ spreads (bid-ask) which he deemed untradeable—he declined the setup entirely despite "textbook" technical setup (two candles of topping tails suggesting reversal), recognizing cold-market axiom: when spreads widen, short selling intensifies and buyers abandon, creating flush-and-fail cycles. PRFX (Israeli company, recent reverse split, room to 200-MA) became his deep-dive case study in market-spoofing detection: the stock squeezed from $4 to $7.82 in 20 seconds (100% move, generating +$5,970 profit on his adds from $4.15-$7.82), but his detailed level-2 examination revealed spoofing signatures—large orders appearing on MEMX at resistance ($5.30-$5.03), instantly canceling and reappearing below, moving in coordination with price action without execution intent. His thesis: these algorithms depress sentiment, trap retail sellers into premature exits, and occasionally blow up when legitimate catalysts (like TCGL's 400%+ move) overwhelm the spoofed resistance. His decision to scale out aggressively at $7.82 despite observing the stock had room to $10 insider pricing reflected hard-won wisdom: take 100% moves when market structure looks suspicious; don't assume continuation when algorithms are suppressing buying interest.

His scaled exits from $7.00-$8 high to final $6+ close captured ~$5,876 total (+$1 per share averaged). ZC and HOUR provided minor +$200 and +$100 scalps on momentum pops (B-quality setups) before he stepped away. His critical discipline: when sitting -$1,500 early plus +$7K peak, he internally committed "if you get back to green, walk away"—a direct lesson from Tuesday where he'd recovered to +$50K, chased CPSH's halt-up for one final trade, lost $12K, and finished only +$29K instead of the potential $41K+. By 10:30a.m., green $5,876 with no more quality setups visible (light holiday volume, wide spreads on small movers), he closed all screens, eliminating temptation. His month-to-date reflection: three consecutive green days post-losses, proving that consistent small days stack faster than revenge-trade blowups, and that walking away while profitable beats sitting and watching screens, triggering FOMO, and inevitably giving back gains.

Ross finished Wednesday up $13,512 despite a frustrating day marked by choppy price action and reduced conviction. BGLC, a historical pop-and-drop stock, squeezed from open through multiple breakout attempts but provided only fragmented entries; Ross entered into the first clean breakout for +$4K unrealized, added on dips, but sold into the chop for only +$1.7K total—the stock's historical pattern of failing to sustain momentum playing out as expected. IMN, a recent reverse split on the watchlist for potential news catalyst squeeze, initially showed promise: he entered at $10.50 as it broke premarket highs, added into the move to $12+, took some profit around $11.98, bought dips, and managed multiple entries/exits capturing +$2K despite the stock ultimately rolling over hard—a full round-trip pattern that highlighted reduced volatility post-catalyst. Ross recognized that while he peaked at +$16K in the session before giving back gains, the discipline of accepting +$13.5K on reduced size after a drawdown week was the real victory. He emphasized market context: in slow markets with fewer quality setups, smaller-sized winning days that avoid additional losses are superior to oversized bets that compound damage. By stopping at his emotional window closure (after early morning momentum faded), he avoided the afternoon chop that would have eroded more gains.

SUMMARY:
Ross recovered to +$32,150 on day three of his comeback week after starting Monday down $58K and Wednesday up $13K. The session demonstrated both disciplined entries on legitimate catalysts and the frustration of false breakouts in choppy conditions. YMAT, a Chinese recent IPO that had popped to $8 premarket, was already pulling back by market open at 7 a.m.; Ross entered conservatively at $5 with 18K shares across multiple adds, riding the stock to $8 high for a +$11.5K scalp—a steady winner that proved recent IPOs still offered opportunity despite increased volatility. PAPL (Papal Technologies) emerged as an afternoon leading gainer; Ross caught the curl-back-up above resistance and exited quickly for +$3.9K, banking gains before momentum faded. However, PBM became the session's emotional crucible: Ross entered aggressively at $8.50 thinking it would sustain breakout energy, immediately lost $5K on a flush, re-entered on perceived bounces, and cycled through three loss trades totaling -$15K (averaging down into each failed breakout, a behavioral deviation he later acknowledged). SGNX, despite being a 100%+ daily gainer, delivered -$3.1K loss on three scalps due to its penny-stock character (poor range per candle despite huge % move). The session's redeeming moment came late: SLXN halted on cancer cell growth inhibition news (97% inhibition data). Ross entered the halt resumption at $15.99 in the dip-rip, scaled out across multiple exits to $21.78 high, netting +$16K—a high-conviction halt trade that rescued the day from underwater. By 12:15 p.m., Ross was at +$32K but fully aware he had given back $30K+ from his $60K+ unrealized peak, had taken excessive size on false breakouts, and urgently needed to preserve capital. He logged off consciously, calling the day a successful recovery session despite poor execution and behavioral warnings (revenge trading, oversizing).

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Wednesday down $5,900 in his second red day of the week, a frustrating session where every single trade was initially green but he either failed to take profit or added into losing positions. Opening strong, CRK gapped up from $7 to $7.85 on open and Ross entered 10K shares at $7.50, scaling to +$2.5K at $7.75. Instead of banking the scalp, he added 5K more shares expecting $8 target, but the stock reversed sharply back to $7.25 and stopped him for -$5K total. This set the emotional tone: one big loss knocked him into the hole immediately. DAR and MTSL provided small wins/losses as he rotated through penny stocks with weaker volume. The session's major tell was LEDS, where he filled 15K shares at $5.00 after manually overriding his share-size warning (meant to cap at 10K). The stock popped to $5.10 (+$1,500 unrealized) but reversed to $4.70 and he forced out at $4.80 for -$4.5K loss. Ross was explicit in his confession: "I thought I lifted my cap back down but apparently I didn't. Max position size 10K shares will be in place tomorrow." Each loss compounded his frustration, and he acknowledged the core behavioral issue—refusing to take small profitable exits (CRK at +$2.5K would have left him +$2.3K on the day), instead pushing for bigger wins that reversed. His IRA account was +6% (+$1,183) on conservative MTSL trading due to Interactive Brokers' 60-second order-fill lag (which frustrated him immensely and prompted consideration of switching brokers), but his main account -$7K loss dominated the session. Despite being up $68K month-to-date and $222K year-to-date, the emotional weight of going from +$2.5K to -$5K on one trade illustrated the struggle of greedy entries and reluctant exits.

Ross finished Wednesday down $1,100 with his third consecutive red day, struggling through choppy conditions and false breakouts that characterized the entire week. VXRT, a premarket-scan gap-and-go on biotech strength, failed to hold the $6.80 resistance he'd targeted; he entered at $6.50 for the break over the half-dollar, saw it tap $6.66 but faded back to $6.00, forcing a -$1.8K loss right at open. VLRX emerged as a high-day momentum scanner hit, spiking from $2.50 to $3.08 premarket, then pulling back to $2.50 by open. Ross entered the scalp at $2.86-$2.97 and $3.20, added aggressively on each pop expecting halt-squeeze continuation, and worked it methodically to $3.45 high for a +$3K total scalp—staying engaged and patient rather than revenge-trading. ANYP's false breakout delivered a minor -$100 consolation loss. His reflection noted this as the third red day in a row (Wednesday, Thursday, Friday), with the week showing $5K cumulative loss against a normally strong month (+$68K year-to-date). However, perspective was critical: his 13-day hot streak prior had generated $79K profit (avg $6K/day), so these three chop days represented only a 10% pullback—within acceptable variance. He emphasized that market conditions had shifted from hot to neutral to slightly cold, requiring size reduction and scalp focus rather than swing-trade conviction on breakouts. Ending conscious at 11 a.m., he avoided afternoon deterioration, demonstrating discipline despite the third consecutive small red close.

His week tally was -$5,600 (starting -$1,500 Thursday, adding -$3,800 Friday), putting him in critical territory psychologically: fourth red day required conscious size reduction and rule application. SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday down $206.67 on his fifth consecutive red day, marking an exhausting stretch that followed a remarkable hot streak of 13 consecutive green days (+$79K total, ~$6K daily average). Coming off previous days of declining performance (Thursday -$3,800, Wednesday -$1,500, Tuesday -$1,100, Monday -$5,900), Friday's tiny loss felt almost trivial numerically but represented a psychological toll. PIXIE, a penny stock that showed early promise with a pop to $9.84 from $9.44 entry, appeared to offer a dip-recovery opportunity after pulling back. UU, a halt-resumption stock on news, showed characteristic choppy behavior: it spiked from $2.50 to $2.64 premarket but halted, then resumed down instead of up, creating a false breakout pattern. Ross entered the dip-rip at $2.95 hoping for the typical round-trip squeeze, but it flushed instead, costing -$200. His reflection noted the meta-pattern: while Friday's -$207 loss was negligible relative to the month (still +$68K month-to-date, +$228K year-to-date), the cumulative psychological weight of five red days warranted serious analysis. He acknowledged conservatively reduced share size (1K-2K shares vs. normal 5K-10K) indicated he was already risk-managing; his IRA account had successfully captured a +6% gain despite Interactive Brokers' execution lag frustration. His core insight was critical: "The reason I'm losing right now isn't because the stocks that I'm getting into aren't working. It's because of the way I'm trading them—I was trading more conservatively in my IRA quicker to take profit, more aggressively in my main account and as a result giving back profit." Monday would require market closure; trading would resume Tuesday with clear positioning: he needed to emerge from this drawdown reset and rebuild confidence through disciplined, smaller-sized scalps.
