SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday down $4,311 in Martha's Vineyard during day 8 of his trading challenge, representing his first red day of the break (+$20K over first seven days, -$10K over past two days total). The session began with promise: he was up +$600 mid-morning on JAM (pullback trades capturing +$5 profit before a sharp flush), +$800 on AERC (straightforward open momentum scalp), and +$800 on minor wins. However, AUVI became the session killer. Described as having a "really nice move" breaking $2.70 to $3.04 to $3.26, Ross sized aggressively with large position adds expecting the squeeze to accelerate, but the stock sharply reversed and flushed back, trapping him in a -$3K total loss—the single largest loss of the day that flipped him from +$800 to -$2.5K. His self-critique was harsh: "I knew I had a choice I could have taken the profit and taken the $2,500 instead I add I was being more aggressive in hindsight that was not the right move." APBO (pre-market light-volume stock) and PBTS (the desperate revenge trade after AUVI loss) consumed the rest of his attention, with increasingly poor execution as frustration mounted. By 9:45 a.m., he was at +$800 and audibly asked aloud "should I stop right here?" The answer was yes, but he continued, taking three additional trades that turned his +$800 to -$4.3K by late morning. His meta-commentary was reflective: "This is the definition of overtrading—buying breakouts, not getting breakouts, catching flushes, selling on the flush, getting back in for the breakout, then false breakout, catching another flush." He noted the challenge of the traveling trading setup, explicitly stating: "I'm triply disappointed—I'm disappointed I overstayed, I'm disappointed that after going red I tried to dig out and went further red, I'm disappointed I spent this whole morning here instead of enjoying." By challenge end, he was at +$9.6K cumulative (goal was $9K over 9 days), but Friday's loss meant needing to maintain discipline Saturday (or choose not to trade) to preserve the win.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished down $35,000 in a devastating single-trade loss that defined the session: NURO (neurotech biotech) had news catalyzing a gap-up to $10.30 premarket and strong consolidation suggesting potential for further squeeze. He entered at $10.30 thinking he had the right pullback entry, added aggressively at $11-$11.25 expecting breakout to $12+, and the stock initially cooperated, breaking $11.50 to $12, then halting up on circuit-breaker. However, upon resumption, instead of continuing higher (the typical pattern Ross had learned), NURO flushed immediately to $9.93 and halted again—going down. This "unreal" reversal trapped him in a position he couldn't exit during the halt, forcing a massive loss. His post-session footage showed he was up $20K in the early morning before sitting down to that one trade, making the final day outcome down $15K overall. His reflection emphasized the afternoon-trading pattern: he had made $3.6K on NURO earlier in the day and stepped away to run errands (DMV), came back around 3 p.m., saw the stock consolidating beautifully, gave into FOMO ("it looked like a perfect consolidation pattern"), and took aggressively sized position hoping for the repeat move. He explicitly acknowledged this as his biggest struggle as a trader: inability to walk away when a good morning is secured, instead chasing afternoon patterns despite lower accuracy and higher volatility. The 70%+ accuracy he'd earned over thousands of trades didn't prevent the 25-30% of losing trades from appearing "perfect" in the moment. His metrics showed afternoon trading was consistently negative (-$80K+ recorded), yet the positive reinforcement from the few afternoon winners kept him returning. The NURO loss, combined with previous afternoon catastrophes (CARV -$50K earlier), reinforced the critical lesson: discipline to exit the market after a winning morning was not weakness but strength, and the temptation to "just get a little more" resulted in catastrophic single-trade wipeouts.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday down $37,000 in one of his toughest days, caught between fighting market conditions and poor personal execution. ZEVO, the leading gapper despite his awareness of shelf-registration filing risk (125M secondary offering pending), became his first trade. He knew the risk going in but traded it anyway, entering 7.5K shares at $7 for the break, stopping out quickly at $6.50 for -$6K, then re-entering on perceived bounce at another loss, and a third loss attempt. His meta-commentary was honest: "I knew it had a shelf registration which created risk and so my very first trade on that I was down 6 thousand bucks then I took another trade and lost on that and I was down 9 thousand then I took another trade on zevo lost again." By the third loss, he was already -$12K and knew he was in emotional free-fall. ACY (light-volume but momentum-spiking stock) became the catastrophic finale: emerging after the morning setup window closed, ACY spiked from $5.85 to $6.63, and Ross entered aggressively on the one-minute breakout expecting continuation. Instead, it reversed sharply, dropped to $6.30 low, and halted down—at which point he was trapped and forced to absorb the full -$25K position loss (15K-17K share size at 25-30 cent reversal). His closing reflection was stark: "I'm down $37K today I'm disappointed but at the same time it's really like one good day of trading. So that's where I'm at right now." By noting that one strong day would recover the loss, he was already mentally compartmentalizing, but the emotional weight was evident. The week had started -$37K Monday, recovered +$55K Tuesday, +$6K Wednesday, but Friday's -$37K meant he was down $20K on the week overall. His final discipline: recognizing the danger of consecutive oversized losses that encourage even larger desperation bets, he chose to stop trading, prepare mentally for Monday, and acknowledge that trading when frustrated and after losses had led him into exactly this trap before.

By mid-day (noted as day 17 of 2018, likely mid-January), Ross was forced to confront the pattern: he was red on 3 out of 5 trades (60% loss rate vs. his usual 70%+ win rate), accuracy was degraded, and despite being up $82K-$83K on the month and $212K year-to-date, the emotional weight of the $2.8K daily loss was outsized. Ross finished down $6,000 on a choppy day where the market lacked clean, followable action, forcing him to trade eight different stocks with mixed results. His biggest winner was CEL-Z (+$3,500), where he executed multiple scalps and captured the momentum surge up to $4.44, but even there he top-ticked by adding at the high. His largest loss was DATS at -$8,000, a position where he was correct about the setup (first five-minute candle making a new daily high) but mistimed the exit by holding through the third pullback, which failed badly. He then panic-traded several smaller positions: FRGE (+$700), DRTS (+$129), and LL (-$450) were minimal positions that reflected his scattered focus. LLAP cost him -$2,300 on a chop trade, and ALLG (a daily breakout play that he later recognized was outside his usual price comfort zone) resulted in a -$3,400 loss. His commentary was brutally honest: he traded eight stocks instead of staying focused on the one obvious leader, and the market conditions were "choppy" with topping tails and poor follow-through throughout. Most critically, after the -$3,400 loss on ALLG, he was down $7,000 and immediately jumped into DATS trying to recover—the classic emotional cascade that doubles losses. He recognized his oversizing and overtrading, realizing he had simultaneously reduced share size (from max to current levels) yet was still taking too many low-probability trades. His commitment to scale further down to 6,000 shares and enforce stricter discipline going forward indicated he understood the severity of his behavioral breakdown.

Ross finished down $13,800 in what he described as his most frustrating day in recent trading history, despite starting the session up +$2,500. His commentary revealed the core psychological trap: each loss made him slightly more desperate, slightly more willing to add to underwater positions, and slightly less disciplined about size. He explicitly stated his max loss is $5,000 and discussed how tempted he was to keep trading to make back the losses, but he stopped himself from that spiral. His closing reflection was about the "luck of the draw"—trading had felt right but execution was wrong, timing was wrong, and management was catastrophic.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished March down $18,000, marking the month's final day as one of his worst. He entered VVUS (gapping up 300% from $1-$5 pre-market) with initial conviction—buying the first one-minute candle making a new high at ~$4—but his aggressive add-ons at higher prices ($4.25, $4.35, $4.40) proved catastrophic. After selling half at $4.50 for +$2,000 profit, he added back "revenge-style" with oversized shares expecting a rip to $5, but instead the stock flushed back to $3.80, costing him a massive net -$5,000. This was the pivotal trade that set the tone for his emotional downward spiral for the rest of the day. SGPX provided some recovery (+$1,000), with the stock spiking from $5.30 all the way to $10.50 after-hours, but by that point he was already deep in the red. His self-analysis was brutally honest: he had increased his normal share size to 3,000 shares on VVUS (instead of his typical discipline), clicked the buy button four times and ended up with 12,000+ shares, and this massive position sizing turned a recoverable loss into a devastating one. He made an explicit commitment: resizing from his 20,000-share max down to 6,000 shares for April, disabling P&L tracking to reduce emotional reactivity, and enforcing a rule that after any red day over $5K he would scale down the next day. His final commentary reflected the psychological burden: he'd gone from being up significantly on the month to finishing down, carried this baggage into the next month (April), and recognized his biggest problem wasn't strategy but discipline and rule-following. He noted that even his students, who are more disciplined than he is, likely out-earn him despite his higher conviction and size.

THMO provided a series of small scalps that offset losses but failed to push the day into the green. The underlying issue was dispersed attention—he was juggling bank stock trades (higher risk, volatile spreads) alongside small-cap scalps, and neither provided clean follow-through. He ended by walking away deliberately rather than pushing harder, recognizing that today was not a day to be aggressive.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Thursday down $656 in what he called "a ride on the roller coaster"—specifically RENT, which dominated his entire session. This was a textbook volatility play: RENT had gapped up 300% overnight and continued moving in the pre-market, and Ross traded it six separate times across the entire morning. His first entry was clean (+$400), but his second entry at the open with oversized shares (2,500 when he meant 500) broke his protocol and started a cascade of losses. By mid-morning, he had reached his low point of -$4,600 down, facing his daily max-loss limit of $5,000. Rather than panic or give up, he managed his emotional state, recognized that the stock was consolidating above its moving average, and took a smaller re-entry at $24.35 with 1,000 shares. When RENT ripped from $24.35 to $28.90 after a halt-resume, he captured +$1,400 and recovered to nearly break-even. His self-analysis was honest: he increased his normal position sizing to 3,000 shares on RENT (his max is 5,000 until building a cushion), clicked buy four times, and ended up with 12,000+ shares at peak—and that excessive sizing almost cost him his max-loss rule. ALRM provided a tiny +$23 scalp. Ross ended by committing to tighter discipline for April: reducing his max position to 6,000 shares and enforcing a rule that days over -$5,000 losses trigger the next day's sizing down.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Friday down $1,928, the last trading day of a difficult week. This was the second consecutive red day; he had given back $8,400 the prior day in what he called an "avoidable" loss (overstaying a winning position). Today he took three trades in the morning window (7–10am, his most profitable zone) and deliberately walked away when his window closed, resisting the temptation to chase recovery. REVE was a pre-market gapper he added on aggressively through the squeeze, pushing his cost basis from $14.50 to $15.50, but the stock flushed hard to $13.21 on sellers—he cut with a -$1,200 loss. CYTO was his recovery trade: a wild, choppy high-day-momo stock with big spreads and frequent reversals, but he managed to scale in aggressively ($3.50 entry, adds at $4.25, peak to $4.60) and captured +$1,200 by selling into rejection. AMAM was a small daily setup test that failed immediately for -$100. By the end of his window, he was close to break-even and chose to lock in the -$1,928 rather than gamble for recovery on a slow Friday before a weekend. His bigger lesson: yesterday he was down $2,000 at this point and overstayed, doubling the loss to -$8,400; today he chose discipline and stopped early.

Ross finished Tuesday down $1,500 in what he termed a "choppy week" following the market's 1,200-point drop the prior day. Leading gappers were weak (only 38% on the best candidate AUVI), signaling a challenging market. He noted that being down $3,600 before this final trade was enough of a signal to walk away; instead, he tried one more trade hoping for a big winner. SUMMARY:
Ross finished day 27 of the 100K Challenge down $4,044.53, his second red day in the challenge. This was a day where he was red on three of four stocks and acknowledged that being down on the first two trades within 30 minutes should have been a clear signal to stop—but he continued, digging himself deeper. AEHR had news and opened strong, but he bought high (into the exhaustion) and the stock reversed immediately, costing -$918. CX looked promising with the gap-and-go setup, but despite being up $4,000 on 10,000 shares at the $7.50 high, he couldn't hold above that level and ended with only a +$600 scalp. SKYS had after-hours news that he was nervous about given light volume and big spreads, and it failed immediately for -$2,100. RESN was the worst: he saw a 40,000-share seller and anticipated the seller moving, which it did. He jumped in with 10,000 shares at $5.00 and added another 2,500, reaching an average of $5.03 and expecting an easy run to $5.50+ and the whole-dollar break. Instead, the stock dropped from $5.09 to $4.68 instantly—he was down $4,400 at worst. He managed to hold through consolidation above the 9-moving-average, thinking it would return to $5.10, but the stock eventually flushed and he exited at $4.65 for a -$3,400 loss. Ross admitted he shouldn't have taken this trade with big size while already down; he also noted that when his accuracy drops to 25% (1 win out of 4 trades), it's a signal the market isn't on his side.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished day 36 of the 100K Challenge down $3,930.01, his sixth red day of the challenge. Despite being down $4,044 early, he recovered significantly from his low point through disciplined position management. PM was his only clean winner: a pre-market resistance break that netted a clean +$720 scalp without stress. SGY was the most frustrating: he started with 5,000 shares at $6.90, added another 5,000 on a micro-pullback at $7.30 for an average of $7.08, saw the stock spike to $7.50 (up $4,200 on the position), but then got flushed hard on a single one-minute candle down to $7.08 (breakeven) and eventually to $6.92. He exited near the low at -$2,100. ZZZ and PHMD were small losses (<-$1,120 combined) from stocks that simply lacked follow-through. AAOI was a reversal trade where he had the right idea but wrong timing—the stock had fresh sellers and no buyers, and his 2,000-share position got caught in a -$1,000 move. Ross reflected that being red on four of five names at 25% accuracy indicated the market wasn't on his side; while he had the right setups, execution and follow-through were absent. He emphasized that risk management (5,000-share max until hitting $1,000 cushion) prevented catastrophic losses today. His account remained up $226,000 on the month despite this red day, and he was still ~$28,000 from the 100K goal, on pace to hit it in March.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished February 1st, the second red day in a row, down $7,381. This day exemplified a market with no follow-through—every stock that popped on the opening simply reversed back down, a pattern that forced him into poor trades. DCIX was the opener: a gap-and-go setup with news that he anticipated would break through the half-dollar, but it reversed immediately for -$1,700. BURG had been called out in his chat room and looked promising with an initial move from $4.20 to $4.57 (+$3,000 on his 10,000-share position), but instead of scaling out he added more shares at $4.57, and the stock then flushed all the way down to $3.74, turning his $3,000 winner into a -$4,600 loss. This was the day-killing trade. He recovered partially with BSPM: a fresh gap-scanner candidate that he caught at $4.30 and saw spike to $4.75 (+$1,500 profit taken), but then re-entered on a dip and got caught on the second flush for -$1,000. The overarching issue was market structure: gap-scanner candidates were opening and gapping down (OPGN), and high-day-momo stocks were popping once on fresh news but lacking any second leg of continuation. Ross noted that on days like today, the best trade is waiting for the first pullback and buying that, not the initial spike. His accuracy was only 40% (2 wins, 3 losses), and the P&L losses were disproportionately large because he traded with 10,000 shares—volatility that works fine on 30-cent sustained moves but is devastating on fake-outs and reversals.

Ross finished the second day of the month down $1,300 on four trades, reflecting a choppy market with poor liquidity and wide bid-ask spreads that made it difficult to execute cleanly. BRTX and ONCS were minor scalps (+$164 and +$72 respectively) on stocks that moved briefly but with choppy price action. Overall, Ross noted that while keeping red days small was important, he was also getting a bit antsy late in the day, which led him to take the RKDA trade despite his better judgment. By late morning, recognizing the lack of momentum and the strain of trading in poor conditions, he stepped aside rather than chase further setups.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished the second red day of the year down $9,300 on four trades, his worst start to a month yet but still small in isolation. The day was dominated by BNAI, a $28+ stock he initially watched skeptically due to its high price, but when it spiked up to $36 in pre-market on news of terminating a $50 million equity purchase agreement, he got in at $33.81 and rode the squeeze to an initial +$8,000 profit. Rather than take profits on the entire position when it hit $36-$36.80, he held most of the position, added back on the next squeeze through $38, and then got caught by a massive seller (likely institutional): the stock dropped from $36+ all the way to $33 in one violent candle, stopping him out for a -$17,000 loss on that add-on, reducing his cumulative BNAI profit to near zero. He correctly noted that when he was down $3,000 early in the day, he became overly conservative on subsequent trades—a psychological response that limited wins but also protected against cascading losses. ARNA provided small scalps (+$1,700 total) with multiple micro-entries against big ask sellers, while RVSN was a Hail Mary trade: a recent reverse-split Israeli tech stock that popped on news and looked to have room to the 200-moving average, but it reversed just as sharply, costing him -$3,000. Most significantly, Ross made the mature decision to stop trading at 8:30 a.m. after being down $9,300, resisting the temptation to swing for the fences to dig himself out. He finished the week up $70,700, and his perspective on the importance of walking away early and managing risk on bad days showed growth from his early trading career.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished the third red day of the month trading from New York City, down $3,182 on five trades. Despite being on the road, he had strong geographic context and kept the daily loss small. ADIL was the gap scanner's leading gapper but suffered an immediate reversal off the first candle's new high, costing -$1,000 on his initial 5,000-share entry, but he recovered +$75 on a second micro-entry lower. YEN was the day-killer: a high-day-momo stock that squeezed from $2.89 to $3.17, and he aggressively added at $3.09, $3.15, and $3.27 with 10,000 total shares expecting a halt-driven squeeze to $3.50+, but instead it reversed sharply to $2.70 in one violent minute, stopping him out for -$2,300. This trade exemplified his vulnerability to overconfidence on higher-volatility plays: he anticipated a halt that never came, and the stock's sudden reversal caught him holding maximum size. NFEC was partially redeemed with +$300 on a similar halt-resume setup that actually worked, and MRI was a near-breakeven trade (+/-$78 loss) on a halt-resume that closed below the open. Ross reflected that while he was averaging about $2,139/day for the year, he had given back 1.5 good days' worth of profit; he acknowledged the frustration of seeing YNOW eventually break higher to $4.80+ after his exit, illustrating the constant tension between capital preservation and missing winning trades. He emphasized remaining focused on December's goal and that red days, while disappointing, remained accountable-practice opportunities.

SUMMARY:
Ross started August, the beginning of a new month, with a red day, down $4,490. While disappointing to begin a month in red, he noted historically strong Augusts (2016: $32,000; 2017: $6,300; 2018: $86,000) and January (started -$4,200, finished +$117,000), so an early red day was not an omen. XIII was the primary loss: a gap-scanner candidate with fresh news that spiked down to $1.80 on a one-minute dip and then bounced, creating a false-breakout setup. Ross got in at $1.82-$2.17 with 10,000 shares, targeting a move to $2.50+ based on a pullback-then-breakout pattern, but every breakout attempt (at $2.17, $2.21, $2.28, and $2.36) failed, and he gave back all profits for a -$3,000 loss. The frustration was partly psychological: each breakout should have worked, yet each failed in sequence, leaving him increasingly frustrated and vulnerable to impulsive trades. MYO was a Hail Mary after being down $3,000: he saw a headline pop in a stock he didn't fully understand, jumped in at $2.46-$2.49 for 10,000 shares, and it immediately reversed to $2.30, costing -$1,500. Ross correctly identified this as a violation of his own rules: trading after three consecutive losses. He stopped himself from forcing a fourth trade, recognizing that when the market is choppy and setups fail repeatedly, the best trade is no trade. He was up $360,000 on the year and was not in drawdown territory, so taking a small red day and walking away was the mature decision. He emphasized the importance of trading the market that exists, not the one he wants, and that some days simply lack follow-through regardless of setup quality.

SUMMARY:
Ross completed day 1 of his December trading rehab (trading-focused discipline challenge) down $1,800 on one stock (SYS) across four separate entries. The rehab rules included: (1) 2,000-share maximum per position (enforced by broker), (2) $1,000 daily max-loss circuit breaker, (3) three-consecutive-loser stop rule. He had initially been called out by viewers for trading an exempted TD Ameritrade account on the same day, and he acknowledged this undercut the spirit of the rehab—starting this day forward, he committed to trading only his Lightspeed account with the stated restrictions. SYS gapped up 119% on the news with a 2.6M share float and 400K pre-market volume, meeting most of his criteria. The bell rang and immediately broke $6.50, squeezing to $6.70, then pulled back to consolidate. Ross waited calmly for a second five-minute candle to break, stayed disciplined, and bought at exactly $7.00 with his 2,000-share position for the break of $7.09. It hit $7.14 and dropped to $6.95, triggering his stop at $6.65 for -$293. He re-entered at $6.70 and quickly made +$500 on the recovery. A third entry at $7.09 broke even. His fourth and final entry at $7.13-$7.25 (2,000 shares) was aggressive, anticipating a break of the half-dollar that would trigger a rip to $7.50+, but instead the stock flushed to $6.75, triggering both his third consecutive loser rule AND his max-loss rule (down $1,000+). Despite the red day, he noted he followed all his circuit-breaker rules and that his only mistake was overconfidence on the final trade—a good lesson in managing emotions when holding full rehab-sized position in a volatile move.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished the week (Friday of a strong week) up $2,435 on two trades in a hot market with parabolic momentum stocks including ARKD (up to $66), DXR, ZZSH, and others. He reflected on trading philosophy: breakout traders like himself buy near all-time highs and have tight 1:1 profit/loss ratios, requiring 60%+ accuracy to be profitable (he runs 68-70%); support-and-resistance traders (like his friend Mike) target 2-3:1 ratios but move slower. AMTX was a pre-market gap play where he got in at $2.59, added at $2.64-$2.65 with 10,000+ shares, and saw the stock spike to $2.99 at one point; he did NOT hold the entire move and instead scaled out at various resistance points, taking +$1,800 despite the stock eventually pulling back to $2.02—a disciplined partial-exit strategy that prevented giving back profits but also left money on the table, a classic breakout-trader tension. KBSF was a follow-up red-to-green play: bought at the dip/reversal, added at higher prices, and he profited +$600 on multiple exits. Ross acknowledged that his fear of holding big winners through pullbacks (which sometimes reverses them entirely) drives him to take quick profits, keeping his average winners small (~6 cents/share on AMTX despite large position size). He's up $462,777 on an account he started with $583 in January 2017, approaching his $500K halfway goal toward his $1M target, and noted that yesterday's $4,000+ red day followed by today's +$2,435 illustrates the ebb-and-flow nature of trading—big wins and losses cluster together, and the goal is to simply make larger wins than losses over time.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished day 6 of his red streak down $3,000, his sixth consecutive red day. While frustrating, he was not at his lowest historical drawdown (-$53,000 in November), and he committed to turning it around. INO was the primary loss: a $2.60+ stock gapping up 119% with 2.6M float that showed pre-market consolidation, and when the bell rang, it immediately broke the half-dollar and squeezed to $9.70. Ross got in at $8.95 with 12,000 shares, saw it hit $9.70 (up $9,000 on the position), then added more at $9.16 and $9.30, filled partially at $9.25 and sold 4,000 of those shares for +$2,200 profit (first-in-first-out accounting). He was holding 2,000 shares bought at $11.00 (the add) that then flushed to $10.00, and he stopped out for a net -$1,600 loss (despite earlier partial gains of +$2,200). The stock then broke 11+ and ran to $12.30+. Ross identified this as a failure to execute on an ABCD setup: he needed to wait for the pullback and buy there, not chase breakouts upward. SLS cost -$58 on 1,000 shares, another quick loss. SYS cost -$1,000 after he had been down $800 and was looking for one more trade to break even—precisely the emotional decision-making he's been fighting against. Throughout the recap, Ross analyzed his red streaks (March, June, October 2019, November 2019) and identified common threads: FOMO entries, overconfidence, impatience, not waiting for pullbacks, being too aggressive after losses. He reflected that his biggest mistakes were buying extended pre-market charts anticipating squeezes without waiting for pullbacks, and then doubling down when they didn't work. His worst trade that week was on XVI (another halt-resume add trade that halted going down), exemplifying the vulnerability of aggressive adding into halts. He committed to: (1) trading fewer stocks, (2) trading pullbacks instead of breakouts, and (3) trading with smaller share sizes tomorrow.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished the second red day of his red streak down $3,600 on four trades, having over-traded in a volatile market and struggled with chart loading issues that added to frustration. The day was marked by the worst kind of volatility: multiple stocks moving simultaneously, making it impossible to focus clearly and leading to poor execution. HPIQ was the destructive trade: pre-market it had a high of $68, which collapsed during the market's weakening, and Ross tried a bottom-bounce strategy on a halt-down resumed play. He got in around $34 early, then added more at $31+ expecting a bounce back toward $40+, but the bounce was weak and it sold off hard through his entry, stopping him out for -$4,500 (one of the worst trades of the streak). This violated his own rule of not chasing stocks and of scaling cautiously on halt-resumes; instead, he had been aggressive, and the stock's inverse relationship with the market (selling off as the S&P bounced, and bouncing as the S&P sold off) made it difficult to time. ALT and PROR provided small wins (+$1,600 each) that offset about half the damage. Ross lamented that chart-loading issues (slow refresh on his platform) forced him to jump from level-2 screen to level-2 screen without clear position visualization, creating overwhelm and dizziness. He traded 10+ stocks in a day, which was unsustainable for one person. By end of day, he was down $3,600, having burned through a lot of commissions and given back most of yesterday's gains. He was finishing the month around $65,000-$75,000 profit but was aware the last two days had been emotionally draining and indicated he'd been over-trading in choppy market conditions.

SUMMARY:
Ross finished Monday down $12,000 across all four of his active trading accounts (Lightspeed main, Lightspeed retirement, E-Trade, TD Ameritrade IRA), marking his second consecutive red day after a hot streak the previous week (up through Thursday). He was actively learning new trading platforms (E-Trade and TD Ameritrade) while maintaining his core Lightspeed accounts, and this multi-account approach hampered his focus. BIMMY was the primary culprit: a $2.60+ stock gapping up 28% on news with strong pre-market consolidation that he was watching closely. The bell rang, it immediately broke $6.50 and squeezed toward $7.00-$7.30, and he got in at $7.15 (TVkorswim account first), then added at $7.25 expecting a break through $7.50 and move to $8.00. Instead, the stock flushed back to $6.85, stopped him out at $6.58 for a near -$1,000 loss on that first add. He was frustrated because he didn't know what his average cost was (message popups were blocking his view), couldn't see current prices, and platform hotkeys weren't configured correctly. After working through these technical issues and getting hotkeys set up properly, he did get a partial recovery entry at $7.10-$7.20 and made +$400, but the net loss on BIMMY across all four accounts amplified to -$5,200+. The fact that he replicated the same setup failure in all four accounts simultaneously meant that either he had won big (or lost big) with 4x leverage, and today it cost him dearly. He resolved to use only Lightspeed tomorrow to avoid divided attention and noted he'd need to wire funds to the TD Ameritrade IRA account (which dropped below PDT level at $2,100) to trade it further. Despite the red day, he kept perspective: this was product testing, not his core results, and his real focus was on the two main Lightspeed accounts that he reports publicly on his website.

Ross finished his 39th consecutive green day with a strong profit of $12,000, demonstrating the power of limiting losses and taking disciplined profits. PIXIE was the primary driver: a recent reverse-split gapper that had squeezed from $15 to $44 the day prior, creating a natural dead-cat bounce setup on the open. News catalyzing pancreatic cancer treatment broke at 8:00 AM, combining with the bounce to create a powerful squeeze from $14 up to $28. SHPH and RIVN were secondary efforts that resulted in small losses totaling $2,179 net. The lesson for the day was consistency: Ross is focused on 39 consecutive green days by keeping losses small (under 1% when possible) and walking away with profits once market conditions shift. With $44,000 in profits through September 8, he is tracking at 45% of his $100,000 monthly goal.

His initial trade captured the move from $24.50 to nearly $28 per share, netting roughly $22,000 at peak. However, after giving back some profit on subsequent pullbacks, he re-entered near the highs at $31.40 expecting another leg higher toward $35, adding for the breakout. Instead, the stock jack-knifed sharply downward to $28.40, wiping out approximately $16,000 in unrealized gains on that single trade. He cut losses immediately rather than hoping for a recovery. The day was complicated by travel, distractions (phone calls regarding a European flight), and FOMO from a missed trade opportunity earlier in the session. While the catalyst and float were less ideal than his typical setups, and the price action became choppy after the initial clean move, the stock remained the most obvious play available. The lesson: even when leading winners offer tantalizing profits, giving back gains late in the session is painful but manageable when risk is cut quickly.

SUMMARY:
Ross did a second recap for the day, having recovered from early losses to finish green on PGY despite almost blowing the entire day. He opened with three small winners on TBLT, KAVL, and CSIGA, building to roughly +$1,800 early in the session. He then traded FAZE at the open, expecting continuation momentum after it had squeezed in pre-market, but the stock reversed sharply and he stopped out for -$1,100, leaving him up only ~$700 and feeling frustrated. After giving back 75% of his morning gains across three consecutive losses, he did his first recap feeling beaten down. However, when PGY began squeezing at mid-morning, he re-entered aggressively to chase the red-to-green move, risking that his cautious approach might result in missing another opportunity. He bought at $11.25-$11.45, added for the break of $12.00, and caught the breakout up to $12.20+ before the stock reversed. The trade itself was clean and profitable at +$3,200, but the bigger issue was the behavioral lapse: he violated his own rule of walking away after three losses and giving back two-thirds of his gains. By taking that aggressive late-morning trade, he put the entire day at risk and happened to get rewarded, which reinforced poor discipline. The session highlighted the temptation to chase momentum after losses.

SUMMARY:
Ross had his third red day of the month (and second in a row), down $1,989 after reducing his risk following the prior day's $10,000 loss. In an effort to control damage, he had removed his buy button hotkey the day before and capped position size at 10,000-20,000 shares (reduced from prior levels). FRGT, the leading gapper up over 300%, was technically a fine setup, but his order to sell at the open was rejected with a "risk check failed" message from his broker—the hotkey offset was 10 cents, which exceeded 10% of the stock price on a penny stock. While he had successfully scaled out of pre-market trades on the same stock without error, the open rejection trapped him as the stock halted down, wiping out his morning gains and putting him red $1,000 on the trade. C4, his second trade, was a $6 breakout attempt where he sized up aggressively expecting a continuation, but violent rejection sent him down another -$989 as he gave back profits rather than taking them when available. The red day was mitigated by the discipline of stopping at max daily loss ($5K cap) rather than pushing further into drawdown territory. Key lesson: external factors (broker risk checks on penny stocks, wrong position sizing on plays outside his wheelhouse) can sabotage trading regardless of strategy.

KODK had gapped up 50% on news that it had hired a law firm to review executive stock option practices in relation to a government contract award, though he remained skeptical of the catalyst since the SEC investigation was still ongoing. He cautiously entered only after a dip near the open, buying around $10.00-$10.10, then scaling out of the position at higher levels as the stock moved up toward $10.70. He executed 11 total trades across the day (eight winners, three losers) with small base hits averaging 10-20 cents per share—exactly the conservative approach needed to rebuild confidence after drawdown. His largest winner was roughly 18 cents per share; his largest loser was 6 cents. Rather than pushing for bigger profits on what could have been a $2,800+ day by holding larger positions, he locked in +$1,100 and stopped trading, recognizing that this phase of recovery requires patience and consistency rather than aggression. The green day marked the critical shift from "bleeding" to "stopping the bleeding," essential psychology for eventual recovery.
